Process files for metadata, push to MAM, copy files to another S3 bucket. ✅ C File Gateway with Rekognition 🎯 File Gateway provides seamless integration with existing MAM system while enabling real-time processing with Rekognition. Tape Gateway can't process files in real-time from Glacier. ⚠️ A Kinesis Video Streams — Requires major MAM changes, designed for streaming not archived files ⚠️ B Tape Gateway — Can't process files real-time from Glacier archive ⚠️ D Snowball + EC2 — High management overhead with open-source tools vs managed Rekognition 📖 Full Explanation Amazon Rekognition can store information about detected faces in server-side containers known as collections. You can use the facial information that's stored in a collection to search for known faces in images, stored videos, and streaming videos. Amazon Rekognition supports the IndexFaces operation. You can use this operation to detect faces in an image and persist information about facial features that are detected in a collection. This is an example of a storage-based API operation because the service persists information on the server. To store facial information, you must first create (CreateCollection) a face collection in one of the AWS Regions in your account. You specify this face collection when you call the IndexFaces operation. After you create a face collection and store facial feature information for all faces, you can search the collection for face matches. To search for faces in an image, call SearchFacesByImage. To search for faces in a stored video, call StartFaceSearch. To search for faces in a streaming video, call CreateStreamProcessor. AWS Storage Gateway offers file-based, volume-based, and tape-based storage solutions. With a tape gateway, you can cost-effectively and durably archive backup data in GLACIER or DEEP_ARCHIVE. A tape gateway provides a virtual tape infrastructure that scales seamlessly with your business needs and eliminates the operational burden of provisioning, scaling, and maintaining a physical tape infrastructure. You can run AWS Storage Gateway either on-premises as a VM appliance, as a hardware appliance, or in AWS as an Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) instance. You deploy your gateway on an EC2 instance to provision iSCSI storage volumes in AWS. You can use gateways hosted on EC2 instances for disaster recovery, data mirroring, and providing storage for applications hosted on Amazon EC2. Hence, the correct answer is: Integrate the file system of your local data center to AWS Storage Gateway by setting up a file gateway appliance on-premises. Utilize the MAM solution to extract the media files from the current data store and send them into the file gateway. Build a collection using Amazon Rekognition by populating a catalog of faces from the processed media files. Use an AWS Lambda function to invoke Amazon Rekognition Javascript SDK to have it fetch the media file from the S3 bucket which is backing the file gateway, retrieve the needed metadata, and finally, persist the information into the MAM solution. The option that says: Request for an AWS Snowball Storage Optimized device to migrate all of the media files from the on-premises library into Amazon S3. Provision a large EC2 instance and allow it to access the S3 bucket. Install an open-source facial recognition tool on the instance like OpenFace or OpenCV. Process the media files to retrieve the metadata and push this information into the MAM solution. Lastly, copy the media files to another S3 bucket is incorrect. This entails a lot of ongoing management overhead instead of just using Amazon Rekognition. Moreover, it is more suitable to use the AWS Storage Gateway service rather than an EBS Volume. The option that says: Set up a tape gateway appliance on-premises and connect it to your AWS Storage Gateway. Configure the MAM solution to fetch the media files from the current archive and push them into the tape gateway to be stored in Amazon Glacier. Using Amazon Rekognition, build a collection from the catalog of faces. Utilize a Lambda function which invokes the Rekognition Javascript SDK to have Amazon Rekognition process the video directly from the tape gateway in real-time, retrieve the required metadata, and push the metadata into the MAM solution is incorrect. Although this solution uses the right combination of AWS Storage Gateway and Amazon Rekognition, take note that you can't directly fetch the media files from your tape gateway in real time since this is backed up using Glacier. Although the on-premises data center is using a tape gateway, you can still set up a solution to use a file gateway in order to properly process the videos using Amazon Rekognition. Keep in mind that the tape gateway in the AWS Storage Gateway service is primarily used as an archive solution. The option that says: Use Amazon Kinesis Video Streams to set up a video ingestion stream and with Amazon Rekognition, build a collection of faces. Stream the media files from the MAM solution into Kinesis Video Streams and configure the Amazon Rekognition to process the streamed files. Launch a stream consumer to retrieve the required metadata, and push the metadata into the MAM solution. Finally, configure the stream to store the files in an S3 bucket is incorrect. This approach requires significant changes to the existing MAM solution to stream media files directly into Kinesis Video Streams, potentially causing more disruption to the current workflow. Additionally, Kinesis Video Streams is designed for real-time video streaming and processing, which may not be the most efficient approach for processing pre-existing media files stored in a tape library. Lastly, setting up and managing Kinesis Video Streams, configuring Amazon Rekognition integration, implementing a stream consumer, and storing files in S3 introduces additional complexity and management overhead compared to the File Gateway approach.

### Q72
OFICIAL (AIP-C01 profesional): A financial services company creates a multi-account generative AI (GenAI) development environment by using Amazon Bedrock FMs. Employees must authenticate through the corporate Microsoft Active Directory. Employees should only be able to access FMs based on their department assignments. The company requires consistent permissions across AWS accounts. The company requires control over which models each department can use. The solution must ensure Regional resilience for the authentication service. Which solution will meet these requirements with the LEAST operational overhead?
RESPUESTA CORRECTA: Configure AWS IAM Identity Center as the identity federation service and connect to Active Directory. Create permission sets with model access policies that map to departments. Configure multi-account access with Regional failover.
Cartas que YA tienes (otras certs + tu mazo AIP actual; cos = similitud 0-1, TEXTO COMPLETO):
  - [cos=0.573 src=otra-cert] A company uses LDAP for employee authentication . They're releasing a mobile app for employees to access AWS resources with federated access . Requirements: custom-built authentication solution and IAM roles for permissions . Which options should the Solutions Architect implement for authentication and authorization? (Select TWO.) A. Build custom SAML-compatible solution using LDAP for authentication and SAML assertion for IAM identity provider authorization B. Build custom LDAP connector with API Gateway/Lambda for authentication, use DynamoDB for authorization tokens C. Build custom OpenID Connect-compatible solution, use Amazon Cognito Identity Pools for AWS resource authorization D. Build custom SAML-compatible solution for authentication, leverage AWS IAM Identity Center for authorization E. Build custom OpenID Connect-compatible solution with AWS IAM Identity Center ✅ A & C: SAML with LDAP + OpenID Connect with Cognito 🎯 Mobile federation requires either SAML assertions to IAM or OpenID Connect to Cognito Identity Pools for temporary AWS credentials. ⚠️ B — Storing auth tokens in DynamoDB is not recommended security practice ⚠️ D — IAM Identity Center only supports web browsers, not mobile apps ⚠️ E — IAM Identity Center only supports SAML 2.0, not OpenID Connect 📖 Full Explanation AWS supports identity federation with SAML 2.0 (Security Assertion Markup Language 2.0), an open standard that many identity providers (IdPs) use. This feature enables federated single sign-on (SSO), so users can log in to the AWS Management Console or call the AWS API operations without having to create an IAM user for everyone in your organization. By using SAML, you can simplify the process of configuring federation with AWS because you can use the IdP's service instead of writing custom identity proxy code. You can use a role to configure your SAML 2.0-compliant identity provider (IdP) and AWS to permit your federated users to access the AWS Management Console. The role grants the user permissions to carry out tasks in the console. Amazon Cognito provides authentication, authorization, and user management for your web and mobile apps. Your users can sign in directly with a username and password or through a third party such as Facebook, Amazon, Google, or Apple. The two main components of Amazon Cognito are user pools and identity pools. User pools are user directories that provide sign-up and sign-in options for your app users. Identity pools enable you to grant your users access to other AWS services. Amazon Cognito identity pools provide temporary AWS credentials for users who are guests (unauthenticated) and for users who have been authenticated and have received a token. OpenID Connect is an open standard for authentication that is supported by a number of login providers. Amazon Cognito supports the linking of identities with OpenID Connect providers that are configured through AWS Identity and Access Management. The SAML solution allows using company LDAP for authentication with SAML assertions for IAM authorization. The OpenID Connect solution provides SSO functionality for mobile apps with Cognito Identity Pools providing temporary AWS credentials. AWS IAM Identity Center supports single sign-on to business applications through web browsers only, making it unsuitable for mobile applications. Storing authorization tokens permanently in DynamoDB is not recommended - tokens should be temporary with fixed session lengths.
  - [cos=0.583 src=otra-cert] A solutions architect proposes using AWS IAM Identity Center for mobile application authentication with federated AWS access. Why is this approach problematic? A. IAM Identity Center doesn't support SAML 2.0 authentication protocols B. IAM Identity Center only supports web browser-based SSO, not mobile applications C. IAM Identity Center requires separate IAM users for each federated identity D. IAM Identity Center cannot integrate with external identity providers ✅ B. IAM Identity Center only supports web browser-based SSO, not mobile applications IAM Identity Center is designed for enterprise SSO to business applications through web browsers. It lacks the mobile SDK and token-based authentication mechanisms needed for mobile app integration. Mobile apps require different authentication flows and credential management. 🎯 Better approach: Use Amazon Cognito Identity Pools with OpenID Connect or custom SAML IdP for mobile authentication. These services provide mobile SDKs and support token-based authentication suitable for mobile applications.

  - [cos=0.565 src=otra-cert] A company has multiple AWS accounts for different departments (finance, HR, engineering). Each account is managed by Systems Administrators with root access. Requirement: centrally manage policies across multiple AWS accounts to allow/deny specific AWS services for individual accounts or groups of accounts. Which is the most suitable solution with the LEAST complexity? A. Connect all departments by setting up cross-account access to each of the AWS accounts of the company. Create and attach IAM policies to your resources based on their respective departments to control access. B. Use AWS Organizations and Service Control Policies to control the list of AWS services that can be used by each member account. C. Set up AWS Organizations and Organizational Units (OU) to connect all AWS accounts of each department. Create a custom IAM Policy to allow or deny the use of certain AWS services for each account. D. Provide access to externally authenticated users via Identity Federation. Set up an IAM role to specify permissions for users from each department whose identity is federated from your organization or a third-party identity provider. ✅ B. Use AWS Organizations and Service Control Policies 🎯 SCPs centrally control AWS service access across multiple accounts and can restrict even root users, unlike IAM policies which only work within individual accounts. ⚠️ A. Cross-account access — Too complex for multiple accounts, more suited for 2-account scenarios ⚠️ C. Custom IAM Policy — IAM policies can't restrict services across accounts, need SCPs not IAM ⚠️ D. Identity Federation — Focuses on authentication, not service-level policy management across accounts 📖 Full Explanation AWS Organizations offers policy-based management for multiple AWS accounts. With Organizations, you can create groups of accounts, automate account creation, and apply and manage policies for those groups. Organizations enables you to centrally manage policies across multiple accounts, without requiring custom scripts and manual processes. It allows you to create Service Control Policies (SCPs) that centrally control AWS service use across multiple AWS accounts. Remember that AWS Organizations does not replace associating IAM policies with users, groups, and roles within an AWS account. Hence, you still need to set up appropriate IAM policies for your root and member accounts. IAM policies let you allow or deny access to AWS services (such as Amazon S3), individual AWS resources (such as a specific S3 bucket), or individual API actions (such as s3:CreateBucket). An IAM policy can be applied only to IAM users, groups, or roles, and it can never restrict the root identity of the AWS account. By contrast, AWS Organizations lets you use service control policies (SCPs) to allow or deny access to particular AWS services for individual AWS accounts, or for groups of accounts within an organizational unit (OU). The specified actions from an attached SCP affect all IAM users, groups, and roles for an account, including the root account identity.
  - [cos=0.562 src=otra-cert] A company has multiple AWS accounts for different departments (finance, HR, engineering). Each account has a Systems Administrator with root access . The company needs to centrally manage policies to allow or deny AWS services for individual accounts or groups of accounts. Which is the most suitable solution with the LEAST complexity? A. Connect all departments by setting up cross-account access to each AWS account. Create and attach IAM policies to resources based on departments. B. Use AWS Organizations and Service Control Policies to control the list of AWS services that can be used by each member account. C. Provide access to externally authenticated users via Identity Federation. Set up an IAM role to specify permissions for users from each department. ✅ B. Use AWS Organizations and Service Control Policies 🎯 Why correct: Service Control Policies (SCPs) in AWS Organizations are specifically designed for centrally managing permissions across multiple AWS accounts. Unlike IAM policies which only affect users/groups/roles within an account, SCPs can restrict even the root user and affect all identities in member accounts. ⚠️ A. Cross-account access with IAM policies This approach creates significant complexity with multiple cross-account trust relationships and doesn't provide centralized policy management. Each account would need individual IAM policy configuration, defeating the purpose of centralized control. ⚠️ C. AWS Organizations with custom IAM Policy While AWS Organizations is correct, IAM policies cannot restrict services across accounts or affect the root user. Only Service Control Policies (SCPs) can centrally control AWS service access across multiple accounts in an organization. ⚠️ D. Identity Federation with IAM roles This solution addresses user authentication but doesn't solve the core requirement of centrally managing service permissions across multiple AWS accounts. It focuses on federated access rather than service control.
  - [cos=0.573 src=otra-cert] A company is building a client-side JavaScript application that needs to authenticate users through social media accounts and allow them to upload files directly to S3 and write data to DynamoDB tables without exposing AWS credentials in the browser. Which AWS service best addresses this authentication requirement? A. AWS IAM Users with embedded access keys in the JavaScript code B. Amazon Cognito User Pools with Web Identity Federation C. AWS Lambda functions acting as authentication proxies D. Amazon API Gateway with custom authorizers ✅ B. This provides secure temporary credentials through STS AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity without exposing permanent AWS credentials in browser code. ⚠️ A. Embedding access keys in JavaScript exposes credentials to anyone who views the source code - a major security vulnerability. ⚠️ C. Lambda proxies add unnecessary complexity and latency when direct service access is possible with proper authentication. ⚠️ D. API Gateway with custom authorizers doesn't directly solve the social media authentication requirement and adds infrastructure overhead.

### Q73
OFICIAL (AIP-C01 profesional): A company is developing an application by using Amazon Bedrock. Multiple development teams will access the application across different departments. The solution must integrate with the company's existing enterprise identity provider (IdP) for centralized governance. Only approved development teams should be allowed to invoke specific models. The solution must provide an auditable access trail. Which solution will meet these requirements MOST securely?
RESPUESTA CORRECTA: Configure AWS IAM Identity Center with SAML federation from the enterprise IdP. Create permissions sets that restrict Amazon Bedrock model invocation for each OU. Create an SCP that prevents unauthorized actions outside of approved models.

  - [cos=0.74 src=otra-cert] An IT consulting company uses AWS Organizations with multiple accounts grouped into OUs. No damage occurred yet, but they need to implement monitoring for organizational changes . Which actions should be taken to set up proper monitoring? (Select TWO.) A. Create a trail in Amazon CloudTrail to capture all API calls to AWS Organizations. Use Amazon EventBridge and SNS to raise events when administrator-specified actions occur. B. Monitor all changes using Systems Manager and use Amazon EventBridge to notify of any new activity. C. Use AWS Config to monitor compliance of AWS Organizations. CloudTrail + EventBridge/SNS and AWS Config aggregation 🎯 Why correct: CloudTrail captures all Organizations API calls (including unauthorized account additions) while EventBridge can trigger on specific organizational events. AWS Config's multi-account aggregation enables centralized compliance monitoring across the entire organization. ⚠️ B. Systems Manager monitoring Systems Manager is designed for EC2 instances, on-premises servers, and AWS resource configuration management - not for monitoring AWS Organizations structural changes like account additions or OU modifications. ⚠️ D.   - [cos=0.748 src=otra-cert] An IT consultancy uses AWS Organizations to manage multiple AWS accounts across regional offices. A new account was added to an OU and uses an Amazon ECS Cluster with an attached service-linked role . The solutions architect created a custom SCP to deny certain ECS actions for regulatory purposes, but after applying the policy, the account could still perform the restricted actions. Which of the following is the most likely reason for this problem? A. The default SCP grants all permissions attached to every root, OU, and account. To apply stricter permissions, this policy is required to be modified. B. There is an SCP attached to a higher-level OU that permits the actions of the service-linked role. This permission would therefore be inherited by the current OU, and override the SCP placed by the administrator. C. SCPs do not affect any service-linked role. Service-linked roles enable other AWS services to integrate with AWS Organizations and can't be restricted by SCPs. D. The ECS service is being run outside the jurisdiction of the organization. SCPs affect only the principals that are managed by accounts that are part of the organization. ✅ C. SCPs do not affect any service-linked role 🎯 Why correct: Service-linked roles are special IAM roles that enable AWS services to integrate with Organizations and cannot be restricted by SCPs. This is a fundamental limitation of SCPs - they don't affect service-linked roles regardless of how the SCP is configured. ⚠️ A. Default SCP modification This is incorrect because the scenario already states a custom deny SCP was created. The FullAWSAccess default SCP allows all actions, but explicit deny policies override any allow policies, so modifying the default wouldn't solve this issue with service-linked roles. ⚠️ B. Higher-level OU permissions This misunderstands SCP inheritance. Even if a higher-level OU has an allow policy, a deny SCP at the current level would still block actions for regular IAM users and roles. The issue isn't about inheritance but about service-linked roles being exempt from SCP restrictions entirely. ⚠️ D. ECS outside organization This is technically incorrect because the service-linked role was created within the organization by the root account. The ECS service itself isn't "outside" the organization's jurisdiction - the issue is that service-linked roles have special exemptions from SCP restrictions.
  - [cos=0.716 src=otra-cert] An IT consultancy uses AWS Organizations to manage multiple regional AWS accounts. A new account was added to an OU for systems administration. The account uses a root-created ECS Cluster with service-linked role . A solutions architect created a custom SCP to deny certain ECS actions but the account can still perform the restricted actions. Which is the most likely reason for this problem? A. The default SCP grants all permissions attached to every root, OU, and account. To apply stricter permissions, this policy is required to be modified. B. There is an SCP attached to a higher-level OU that permits the actions of the service-linked role. This permission would therefore be inherited by the current OU, and override the SCP placed by the administrator. C. SCPs do not affect any service-linked role. Service-linked roles enable other AWS services to integrate with AWS Organizations and can't be restricted by SCPs. D. The ECS service is being run outside the jurisdiction of the organization. SCPs affect only the principals that are managed by accounts that are part of the organization. ✅ C. SCPs do not affect any service-linked role 🎯 Service-linked roles are AWS-managed roles that enable service integration with Organizations and are explicitly exempt from SCP restrictions. ⚠️ A. Default SCP — scenario states custom deny policy was already created ⚠️ B. Higher-level OU permissions — deny policies override any allow policies in SCP hierarchy ⚠️ D. Service outside organization — service-linked role exists within the organization account 📖 Full Explanation Users and roles must still be granted permissions using IAM permission policies attached to them or to groups. The SCPs filter the permissions granted by such policies, and the user can't perform any actions that the applicable SCPs don't allow. Actions allowed by the SCPs can be used if they are granted to the user or role by one or more IAM permission policies. When you attach SCPs to the root, OUs, or directly to accounts, all policies that affect a given account are evaluated together using the same rules that govern IAM permission policies: – Any action that has an explicit Deny in an SCP can't be delegated to users or roles in the affected accounts. An explicit Deny statement overrides any Allow that other SCPs might grant. – Any action that has an explicit Allow in an SCP (such as the default "*" SCP or by any other SCP that calls out a specific service or action) can be delegated to users and roles in the affected accounts. – Any action that isn't explicitly allowed by an SCP is implicitly denied and can't be delegated to users or roles in the affected accounts. By default, an SCP named FullAWSAccess is attached to every root, OU, and account. This default SCP allows all actions and all services. So in a new organization, until you start creating or manipulating the SCPs, all of your existing IAM permissions continue to operate as they did. As soon as you apply a new or modified SCP to a root or OU that contains an account, the permissions that your users have in that account become filtered by the SCP. Permissions that used to work might now be denied if they're not allowed by the SCP at every level of the hierarchy down to the specified account. As stated in the documentation of AWS Organizations, SCPs DO NOT affect any service-linked role. Service-linked roles enable other AWS services to integrate with AWS Organizations and can't be restricted by SCPs.
